I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the MEX (Minimum Excluded) of a string - the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring
2. If multiple strings exist, we return the lexicographically smallest one
3. Empty string is NOT considered valid
4. We need to check all possible substrings starting from length 1

## Key Insights

1. The MEX must be a string of minimum length
2. We should check in order of increasing length: length 1, then length 2, etc.
3. For each length, we check all possible strings of that length in lexicographic order
4. The first string we find that doesn't appear as a substring is our answer

## Algorithm Steps

1. Start with length 1 strings (single characters)
2. For each possible string of current length in lexicographic order:
   - Check if it appears as a contiguous substring in the input
   - If it doesn't appear, return it as MEX
3. If no length 1 string works, move to length 2, and so on

## Implementation Strategy

1. Generate all possible strings of length 1 in alphabetical order: 'a', 'b', 'c', ...
2. For each string, check if it exists in the input as a substring
3. Return the first one that doesn't exist

## Edge Cases to Consider

- What if length 1 strings all exist? Then we need to check length 2
- The character set is lowercase Latin letters (a-z)

## Step-by-step Solution

```python
def find_mex(s):
    n = len(s)
    
    # Check for strings of length 1
    for c in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        if c not in s:
            return c
    
    # If all length 1 strings exist, check for strings of length 2
    # Generate all possible length 2 strings in lexicographic order
    for c1 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        for c2 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
            substr = c1 + c2
            if substr not in s:
                return substr
    
    # If all length 2 strings exist, check for length 3, and so on
    # But given the constraints, we won't need to go very far
    # The total sum of n <= 1000, so the answer will be found quickly
    
    # For length 3
    for c1 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        for c2 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
            for c3 in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
                substr = c1 + c2 + c3
                if substr not in s:
                    return substr
    
    # This should not happen given the constraints
    return "a"  # fallback

# Read input and solve
t = int(input())
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the MEX (Minimum Excluded) of a string - the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring
2. If multiple strings exist, we return the lexicographically smallest one
3. Empty string is not considered valid (doesn't count as MEX)
4. We need to check all possible strings in lexicographical order until we find one that doesn't exist as a substring

## Approach

1. Generate candidate strings in lexicographical order starting from length 1
2. For each length, generate all possible strings of that length in lexicographical order
3. For each candidate string, check if it appears as a substring in the input string
4. Return the first candidate that doesn't appear as a substring

## Step-by-step Solution

### Step 1: Generate strings in lexicographical order
- Start with length 1: "a", "b", "c", ...
- Then length 2: "aa", "ab", "ac", ..., "az", "ba", "bb", ...
- Continue until we find a string that doesn't appear

### Step 2: Check if substring exists
- For each candidate string, check if it appears in the input string using string slicing

### Step 3: Optimization
- Since the total length of all strings is ≤ 1000, we can safely generate candidates
- We can generate candidates in lexicographical order and stop at the first missing one

## Implementation

```python
def find_mex(s):
    n = len(s)
    
    # Check all possible strings in lexicographical order
    # Start with length 1, then length 2, etc.
    
    # We'll generate strings of increasing length
    length = 1
    
    while True:
        # Generate all strings of current length in lexicographical order
        # Use backtracking to generate all combinations of length 'length'
        
        # First, check if we need to generate all strings of this length
        # We can generate them systematically
        
        # For length 1: 'a', 'b', 'c', ..., 'z'
        # For length 2: 'aa', 'ab', 'ac', ..., 'az', 'ba', 'bb', ...
        
        # Generate all strings of current length
        from itertools import product
        
        for chars in product('abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz', repeat=length):
            candidate = ''.join(chars)
            
            # Check if this candidate appears in the original string
            if candidate not in s:
                return candidate
        
        length += 1
        
        # Optimization: we can reasonably assume that MEX will be found 
        # within a small length (probably <= 5-6 for the given constraints)
        # Since total n <= 1000, we won't need to check too many lengths

# Read input and solve
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    s = input().strip()
    print(find_mex(s))
```

2. If none exist in length 1, we return the first one (which would be 'a')

View Full Response 
I need to find the MEX (minimum excluded substring) of a given string. The MEX is the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring in the input, and if there are multiple such strings, we choose the lexicographically smallest one.

Let me break down the approach:

1. Since we're looking for the shortest substring that doesn't appear in the input, I'll start checking from very short strings.
2. The shortest possible valid MEX strings would be of length 1, then length 2, etc.
3. For each length, I'll generate all possible substrings of that length in lexicographic order and check if they appear in the input.
4. The first (smallest lexicographic) substring of that length that doesn't appear in the input is our answer.

However, there's a key insight: since we want the lexicographically smallest MEX among all shortest MEXs, and we're starting from length 1, we should check all possible strings of length 1 first, then length 2, etc.

Let me trace through the examples:
- For "qaabzwsxedcrfvtgbyhnujmiklop": 
  - Length 1: a, b, c, d, e, f, g, h, i, j, k, l, m, n, o, p, q, r, s, t, u, v, w, x, y, z
  - All letters from 'a' to 'z' appear in the string, so length 1 doesn't work
  - Length 2: We need to check all 2-letter combinations in lexicographic order
  - The first 2-letter combination that doesn't appear would be "ac"

- For "cleanairactbd":
  - All single letters appear, so we need to check length 2
  - "ab", "ac", "ad", ... "bc", "bd", etc.
  - The first 2-letter combination that doesn't appear would be "f" which is actually length 1?

- Does the letter 'f' appear? Looking at the string: c,l,e,a,n,a,i,r,a,c,t,b,d - no 'f'
- All other letters from 'a' to 'e' appear
- So 'f' is the first missing letter (lexicographically smallest missing letter)

For "aannttoonn":
- Does 'b' appear? No - the string has only 'a' and 'n'
- So 'b' is the missing letter

This makes sense! The MEX is the lexicographically smallest string that doesn't appear as a substring. Since single characters are the shortest possible strings (and we don't count empty string), we just need to find the lexicographically smallest character that doesn't appear in the string.

Algo:
1. Check all characters from 'a' to 'z' in lexicographic order
2. Return the first character that doesn't appear in the input string


You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Solve the following coding problem using the programming language python:

I, Fischl, Prinzessin der Verurteilung, descend upon this land by the call of fate an — Oh, you are also a traveler from another world? Very well, I grant you permission to travel with me.

It is no surprise Fischl speaks with a strange choice of words. However, this time, not even Oz, her raven friend, can interpret her expressions! Maybe you can help us understand what this young princess is saying?

You are given a string of n lowercase Latin letters, the word that Fischl just spoke. You think that the MEX of this string may help you find the meaning behind this message. The MEX of the string is defined as the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring in the input. If multiple strings exist, the lexicographically smallest one is considered the MEX. Note that the empty substring does NOT count as a valid MEX.

A string a is lexicographically smaller than a string b if and only if one of the following holds: 

  * a is a prefix of b, but a ≠ b; 
  * in the first position where a and b differ, the string a has a letter that appears earlier in the alphabet than the corresponding letter in b. 



A string a is a substring of a string b if a can be obtained from b by deletion of several (possibly, zero or all) characters from the beginning and several (possibly, zero or all) characters from the end.

Find out what the MEX of the string is!

Input

Each test contains multiple test cases. The first line contains the number of test cases t (1 ≤ t ≤ 1000). Description of the test cases follows.

The first line of each test case contains an integer n (1 ≤ n ≤ 1000) — the length of the word. The second line for each test case contains a single string of n lowercase Latin letters.

The sum of n over all test cases will not exceed 1000.

Output

For each test case, output the MEX of the string on a new line.

Example

Input


3
28
qaabzwsxedcrfvtgbyhnujmiklop
13
cleanairactbd
10
aannttoonn


Output


ac
f
b

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the MEX (minimum excluded substring) of a given string
2. MEX is the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring in the input
3. If there are multiple shortest strings, we choose the lexicographically smallest one
4. The empty string is not considered as valid MEX

## Key Insights

1. We need to check substrings in increasing order of length
2. For each length, we check all possible substrings of that length in lexicographic order
3. The first substring (in lexicographic order) that doesn't appear in the original string is our answer

## Step-by-Step Solution

### Step 1: Generate all possible substrings
- First, create a set of all substring occurrences in the input string
- We need to check substrings of length 1, 2, 3, etc.

### Step 2: Check in lexicographic order
- For length 1: check "a", "b", "c", ... "z"
- For length 2: check "aa", "ab", "ac", ... "az", "ba", "bb", ...
- Continue until we find a substring not in the set

### Step 3: Optimization
- Since the total sum of n over all test cases is ≤ 1000, we can be relatively simple
- We only need to check up to a reasonable length (say 10-15 characters)

## Algorithm

1. For each possible length starting from 1:
   - Generate all possible strings of that length in lexicographic order
   - Check if each string appears as a substring in the input
   - Return the first one that doesn't appear

2. To generate strings in lexicographic order:
   - Use nested loops or itertools.product for small lengths

## Implementation

```python
def solve(s):
    n = len(s)
    
    # Create a set of all substrings of the input string
    substrings = set()
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n + 1):
            substrings.add(s[i:j])
    
    # Check substrings in lexicographic order by length
    # Start with length 1
    length = 1
    
    # Generate all strings of current length in lexicographic order
    # For length 1: a, b, c, ..., z
    # For length 2: aa, ab, ac, ..., az, ba, bb, ...
    
    while True:
        # Generate all possible strings of current length
        # We'll generate them in lexicographic order
        if length == 1:
            for c in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
                if c not in substrings:
                    return c
        else:
            # For length > 1, we need to generate all combinations
            # This is getting complex, let's simplify
            # We can use recursion or iterative generation
            import itertools
            for chars in itertools.product('abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz', repeat=length):
                word = ''.join(chars)
                if word not in substrings:
                    return word
        
        length += 1
    
    return ""  # should never reach here

t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    s = input().strip()
    print(solve(s))
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the MEX (Minimum String that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring) of a given string
2. MEX is the shortest string that doesn't appear as a substring, and if there are multiple, we take the lexicographically smallest one
3. Empty string is NOT considered valid
4. We need to check strings in order of length (starting with length 1), and for each length, check lexicographically

## Approach

1. Start checking from single characters (length 1) and go up
2. For each length, generate all possible strings of that length in lexicographical order
3. Check if each string appears as a substring in the input
4. Return the first string that doesn't appear

## Step-by-step Solution

1. **Length 1**: Check all single characters 'a' to 'z'
2. **Length 2**: Check all two-letter combinations from "aa" to "zz"
3. And so on...

But we can optimize: since we're looking for the shortest non-occurring string, we only need to check lengths starting from 1.

For each length L, we generate strings in lexicographical order and check if they exist in the input.

## Implementation Strategy

1. For length 1: check if any character from 'a' to 'z' appears
2. For length 2: check if any two-character combination appears
3. Continue until we find a missing string

Since the total sum of n is at most 1000, and we're dealing with small strings, this approach is feasible.

```python
def find_mex(s):
    # Convert string to set of all substrings
    substrings = set()
    n = len(s)
    
    # Generate all possible substrings
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n + 1):
            substrings.add(s[i:j])
    
    # Check for MEX starting from length 1
    # For each length, check lexicographically
    length = 1
    
    while True:
        # Generate all strings of current length in lexicographical order
        for c in "abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz":
            # For length 1
            if length == 1:
                candidate = c
                if candidate not in substrings:
                    return candidate
            
            # For length > 1
            else:
                # We need to generate combinations systematically
                # First, generate all combinations of current length
                # We'll build them in lexicographical order
                # This is complex, so let's think differently
                
                # Actually, let's just generate by position
                pass
        
        # This approach is getting complex, let me simplify
        break
    
    # Let me restart with a cleaner approach
    # For each length starting from 1, check all possible strings
    length = 1
    while True:
        # Generate all strings of current length in lex order
        chars = "abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz"
        # Generate all combinations of current length
        # We can do this recursively or iteratively
        candidate = generate_lex_strings(length, chars)
        
        for candidate_str in candidate:
            if candidate_str not in substrings:
                return candidate_str
        
        length += 1

def generate_lex_strings(length, chars):
    """Generate all strings of given length in lexicographical order"""
    if length == 0:
        return [""]
    
    result = []
    for c in chars:
        if length == 1:
            result.append(c)
        else:
            sub_strings = generate_lex_strings(length - 1, chars)
            for sub in sub_strings:
                result.append(c + sub)
    
    return result

def find_mex(s):
    # Generate all substrings of s
    substrings = set()
    n = len(s)
    
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n + 1):
            substrings.add(s[i:j])
    
    # Check length 1 first
    for c in "abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz":
        if c not in substrings:
            return c
    
    # Check length 2
    for c1 in "abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz":
        for c2 in "abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz":
            candidate = c1 + c2
            if candidate not in substrings:
                return candidate
    
    # Check length 3
    for c1 in "abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz":
        for c2 in "abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz":
            for c3 in "abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz":
                candidate = c1 + c2 + c3
                if candidate not in substrings:
                    return candidate
    
    # This shouldn't happen given the constraints
    return "z" * 10

# Read input and solve
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    s = input().strip()
    print(find_mex(s))
```

Model Response

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We need to find the MEX (Minimum EXcluded) of a string - the shortest string that doesn't appear as a contiguous substring.
2. If multiple strings exist, we choose the lexicographically smallest one.
3. The empty string is NOT considered a valid MEX.
4. We need to check all possible strings in order of length, and for each length, check lexicographically.

## Key Insights

1. Since we want the shortest string that doesn't appear as a substring, we should check in order of increasing length:
   - First check all strings of length 1
   - Then all strings of length 2
   - And so on...

2. For each length, we check strings in lexicographical order (a, b, c, ..., z, aa, ab, ac, ...)

3. We can stop as soon as we find the first (shortest) string that doesn't appear in the original string.

4. For length 1: check 'a', 'b', 'c', ..., 'z'
5. For length 2: check 'aa', 'ab', 'ac', ..., 'az', 'ba', 'bb', ... 

## Algorithm

1. Start with length = 1
2. Generate all possible strings of current length in lexicographical order
3. For each string, check if it appears as a contiguous substring in the input
4. Return the first string that doesn't appear

## Implementation Strategy

Since the total sum of n is at most 1000, and strings are short, we can:

1. Create a set of all substrings of the given string
2. Generate candidate strings in lexicographical order
3. Return the first candidate not in the substring set

```python
def find_mex(s):
    # Generate all substrings of the input string
    substrings = set()
    n = len(s)
    
    # Add all contiguous substrings
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n + 1):
            substrings.add(s[i:j])
    
    # Check strings in lexicographical order
    # Start with length 1
    length = 1
    
    # Generate all strings of current length in lexicographical order
    # Use BFS-like approach to generate all strings
    from itertools import product
    
    # For length 1
    for c in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        candidate = c
        if candidate not in substrings:
            return candidate
    
    # For length 2
    for c1, c2 in product('abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz', repeat=2):
        candidate = c1 + c2
        if candidate not in substrings:
            return candidate
    
    # For length 3
    for c1, c2, c3 in product('abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz', repeat=3):
        candidate = c1 + c2 + c3
        if candidate not in substrings:
            return candidate
    
    # If we get here, we need to find a longer string
    # This should be rare given constraints
    # Generate all combinations for length 4
    for c1, c2, c3, c4 in product('abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz', repeat=4):
        candidate = c1 + c2 + c3 + c4
        if candidate not in substrings:
            return candidate
    
    # Should never reach here given the constraints
    return "a"  # fallback

# Read input and solve
t = int(input())

    n = int(input())
    s = input().strip()
    print(find_mex(s))
```

Wait, let me reconsider this. The constraints say the sum of n over all test cases is at most 1000, but we need to be more efficient.

Actually, let me optimize this. Since we're looking for the shortest string, and we check lexicographically, we can generate strings in order and stop at the first missing one.

```python
def find_mex(s):
    # Generate all substrings of the input string
    substrings = set()
    n = len(s)
    
    # Add all contiguous substrings
    for i in range(n):
        for j in range(i + 1, n + 1):
            substrings.add(s[i:j])
    
    # Check strings in lexicographical order by length
    # For length 1
    for c in 'abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz':
        if c not in substrings:
            return c
    
    # For length 2
    from itertools import product
    for c1, c2 in product('abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz', repeat=2):
        candidate = c1 + c2
        if candidate not in substrings:
            return candidate
    
    # For length 3
    for c1, c2, c3 in product('abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz', repeat=3):
        candidate = c1 + c2 + c3
        if candidate not in substrings:
            return candidate
    
    # For length 4
    for c1, c2, c3, c4 in product('abcdefghijklmnopqrstuvwxyz', repeat=4):
        candidate = c1 + c2 + c3 + c4
        if candidate not in substrings:
            return candidate
    
    return "a"  # fallback

# Read input and solve
t = int(input())
for _ in range(t):
    n = int(input())
    s = input().strip()
    print(find_mex(s))
```

    substrings = set()

        candidate = c1 + c2
        if candidate not in substrings:

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Sergey Semyonovich is a mayor of a county city N and he used to spend his days and nights in thoughts of further improvements of Nkers' lives. Unfortunately for him, anything and everything has been done already, and there are no more possible improvements he can think of during the day (he now prefers to sleep at night). However, his assistants have found a solution and they now draw an imaginary city on a paper sheet and suggest the mayor can propose its improvements.

Right now he has a map of some imaginary city with $n$ subway stations. Some stations are directly connected with tunnels in such a way that the whole map is a tree (assistants were short on time and enthusiasm). It means that there exists exactly one simple path between each pair of station. We call a path simple if it uses each tunnel no more than once.

One of Sergey Semyonovich's favorite quality objectives is the sum of all pairwise distances between every pair of stations. The distance between two stations is the minimum possible number of tunnels on a path between them.

Sergey Semyonovich decided to add new tunnels to the subway map. In particular, he connected any two stations $u$ and $v$ that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor, i.e. there exists such a station $w$ that the original map has a tunnel between $u$ and $w$ and a tunnel between $w$ and $v$. You are given a task to compute the sum of pairwise distances between all pairs of stations in the new map.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 200\,000$) — the number of subway stations in the imaginary city drawn by mayor's assistants. Each of the following $n - 1$ lines contains two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n$, $u_i \ne v_i$), meaning the station with these indices are connected with a direct tunnel.

It is guaranteed that these $n$ stations and $n - 1$ tunnels form a tree.


-----Output-----

Print one integer that is equal to the sum of distances between all pairs of stations after Sergey Semyonovich draws new tunnels between all pairs of stations that share a common neighbor in the original map.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
1 2
1 3
1 4

Output
6

Input
4
1 2
2 3
3 4

Output
7



-----Note-----

In the first sample, in the new map all pairs of stations share a direct connection, so the sum of distances is $6$.

In the second sample, the new map has a direct tunnel between all pairs of stations except for the pair $(1, 4)$. For these two stations the distance is $2$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n nodes (subway stations)
2. We add new edges between any two nodes that share a common neighbor in the original tree
3. We need to find the sum of pairwise distances in the new graph

## Key Insights

1. In the original tree, nodes that share a common neighbor are connected by a path of length 2 through that neighbor
2. After adding edges between all pairs that share a common neighbor, we're essentially creating a graph where:
   - All nodes are connected through their common neighbors
   - For any two nodes that don't share a common neighbor (like in a path graph), they might still be far apart

## Analysis of Examples

**Example 1:** Star graph (1 connected to 2,3,4)
- Original: 1-2, 1-3, 1-4
- After adding edges: All pairs share a common neighbor (station 1)
- So all pairs are directly connected (distance = 1)
- Sum of distances = 1+1+1+1+1+1 = 6

**Example 2:** Path graph (1-2-3-4)
- Original: 1-2, 2-3, 3-4
- Pairs that share common neighbors:
  - 1 and 3: both connected to 2
  - 2 and 4: both connected to 3  
  - 1 and 2: already connected
  - 2 and 3: already connected
  - 3 and 4: already connected
- So we add edges: 1-3, 2-4
- Distance between 1 and 4: path 1-2-3-4 (distance 3) OR 1-3-4 (distance 2)
- The shortest path is 2
- All other pairs have distance 1
- Sum = 1+1+1+1+1+2 = 7

## Algorithm

The key insight is that after adding edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, the resulting graph has a specific structure:

1. For any two nodes that don't share a common neighbor, their distance remains 2 (they can be connected through their common neighbor)
2. The distance between any two nodes in the final graph is at most 2

- If they don't have a common neighbor, distance becomes 2 (through their common neighbor)

- If they are at distance > 2: we need to check if they have a common neighbor

- If they share a common neighbor in original tree, they are directly connected
- If they don't share a common neighbor, they must have distance 2

So every pair has distance at most 2 in the final graph.

Therefore, the sum of distances = (number of pairs with distance 1) + 2 × (number of pairs with distance 2)

Pairs with distance 1: all adjacent pairs in original tree, plus all pairs that share a common neighbor after adding edges.

Actually, after adding edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, we have:
- All pairs that share a common neighbor become distance 1
- All other pairs become distance 2

2. Count the total number of pairs
3. The sum = (pairs with dist 1) + 2 × (pairs with dist 2)

Total pairs = n*(n-1)/2
Pairs with dist 1 = pairs that share a common neighbor in original tree

For each node w, if it has degree d, then the number of pairs among its neighbors is C(d,2).

So total pairs with dist 1 = sum over all nodes w of C(degree(w), 2)

Pairs with dist 2 = total pairs - pairs with dist 1

Therefore: sum of distances = (sum of C(degree(w), 2)) + 2 × (n*(n-1)/2 - sum of C(degree(w), 2))

- So 2 pairs have distance 1

If they don't have a common neighbor, their distance becomes 2.

So sum of distances = (pairs that have common neighbor) × 1 + (pairs that don't have common neighbor) × 2

- Total pairs = 6

- (1,3): neighbors of 1={2}, neighbors of 3={2,4}. Common neighbor: 2 ✓

- (2,3): adjacent, common neighbor? 2: {1,3}, 3: {2,4}. Common neighbor: 2 and 3 are neighbors of each other? No common neighbor other than themselves. Common neighbor means a third node. Do 2 and 3 have a common neighbor? 2 is adjacent to 1,3; 3 is adjacent to 2,4. So common neighbor? 2 and 3 don't have a common neighbor.

The pairs with distance 1 are:
- All original adjacent pairs (n-1 = 3)
- All pairs that share a common neighbor (2)

So total distance 1 pairs = (n-1) + (sum of C(degree(w), 2) over all w)

For path graph: 3 + 2 = 5 ✓

Distance 2 pairs = total pairs - distance 1 pairs = 6 - 5 = 1

Sum = (n-1 + C) × 1 + (n*(n-1)/2 - n-1 - C) × 2
= (n-1 + C) + 2×(n*(n-1)/2 - n+1 - C)
= n-1 + C + n*(n-1) - 2(n-1) - 2C
= n-1 + C + n*(n-1) - 2n + 2 - 2C
= n*(n-1) - n + 1 - C
= n(n-1) - n + 1 - C
= n(n-2) - C + 1

For path graph: n=4, 4×2 - 2 + 1 = 8 - 2 + 1 = 7 ✓

For star graph: n=4, 4×2 - 3 + 1 = 8 - 3 + 1 = 6 ✓

Yes! So the formula is: n(n-2) - C + 1 where C = sum of C(degree(w), 2) for all nodes w.

But let me verify the algebra:

Sum = (n-1 + C) + 2×(total_pairs - (n-1 + C))
= (n-1 + C) + 2×(n*(n-1)/2 - n + 1 - C)
= (n-1 + C) + (n*(n-1) - 2n + 2 - 2C)
= n-1 + C + n*(n-1) - 2n + 2 - 2C
= n*(n-1) - n + 1 - C
= n(n-1) - n + 1 - C
= n(n-2) - C + 1

Yes, correct.

## Solution

```python
n = int(input())
if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
    
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Calculate sum of C(degree(w), 2) for all nodes w
    C = 0
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        d = len(adj[i])
        C += d * (d - 1) // 2
    
    # Calculate final sum of distances
    result = n * (n - 2) - C + 1
    print(result)
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n nodes (subway stations)
2. We add new edges between any two stations that share a common neighbor in the original tree
3. We need to find the sum of all pairwise distances in the new graph

## Key Insights

1. In the original tree, any two nodes that share a common neighbor will now be directly connected
2. The new graph will have edges between:
   - All original edges (from the tree)
   - All pairs of nodes that share a common neighbor in the original tree

3. After adding these edges, what happens to the distance between pairs of nodes?

Let me think about this more systematically:

- In a tree, two nodes that share a common neighbor are at most distance 2 apart
- In the new graph, we add direct edges between such pairs
- This means that in the new graph, any two nodes that had distance 2 in the original tree will now have distance 1

So the new graph will have:
- All original edges
- All "distance-2" edges (pairs that share a common neighbor)

## Analysis of Examples

**Example 1**: Star graph (node 1 connected to 2, 3, 4)
- Original: stations 2,3,4 all have distance 2 from each other
- After adding edges: all pairs become directly connected
- Distances: (2,3)=1, (2,4)=1, (3,4)=1
- Sum: 1+1+1 = 3? Wait, that doesn't match output of 6.

I think I'm missing something. Let me recalculate:
- Pairs: (1,2)=1, (1,3)=1, (1,4)=1, (2,3)=1, (2,4)=1, (3,4)=1
- Sum: 6 ✓

**Example 2**: Path graph (1-2-3-4)
- Original: distances: (1,2)=1, (1,3)=2, (1,4)=3, (2,3)=1, (2,4)=2, (3,4)=1
- Pairs that share a common neighbor:
  - 1 and 3 share neighbor 2
  - 2 and 4 share neighbor 3
- Add edges: (1,3) and (2,4)
- New distances:
  - (1,2)=1, (1,3)=1, (1,4)=2 (path 1-3-4 or 1-2-4), (2,3)=1, (2,4)=1, (3,4)=1
  - Sum: 1+1+2+1+1+1 = 7 ✓

## Solution Strategy

1. Build the original tree
2. For each node, identify all pairs of its neighbors that don't already have a direct edge
3. Add these edges to create the new graph
4. Compute the sum of all pairwise distances in the new graph

However, computing distances in a graph with potentially O(n²) edges is expensive.

Alternative insight: In the new graph, the distance between any two nodes is at most 2. Specifically:
- If two nodes are adjacent in original tree → distance 1
- If two nodes are at distance 2 in original tree → distance 1 (due to added edges)
- If two nodes are at distance ≥ 3 in original tree → distance 2 (through the common path)

Wait, let me reconsider. In the new graph, since we add edges between nodes sharing a common neighbor, and in a tree, any two nodes at distance 2 have a common neighbor, then:

- For any two nodes u,v:
  - If d(u,v) = 1: distance = 1
  - If d(u,v) = 2: distance = 1 (due to added edge)
  - If d(u,v) ≥ 3: distance = 2 (since path of length ≥ 3 must pass through some intermediate node)

But actually, if d(u,v) ≥ 3, they might not share a common neighbor directly, but the path goes through intermediate nodes.

Actually, in a tree, if d(u,v) ≥ 3, they don't necessarily share a common neighbor. But the new graph has all edges between nodes that share a common neighbor in the original tree.

Let me think differently. The key insight is that in the new graph:
- Any two nodes that are at distance ≤ 2 in the original tree have distance 1
- Any two nodes that are at distance ≥ 3 in the original tree have distance 2 (because in a tree, any path of length ≥ 3 has at least one node where we can form a common neighbor)

Wait - actually, for nodes at distance 3 in a tree, they don't necessarily share a common neighbor. For example in path 1-2-3-4: distance between 1 and 4 is 3, and they don't share a common neighbor.

But in the new graph, do we add edge (1,4)? Only if they share a common neighbor. In path 1-2-3-4, 1 and 4 don't share a common neighbor (1's neighbor is 2 only, 4's neighbor is 3 only).

So in the new graph:
- Distance between nodes:
  - If d(u,v) = 1: distance = 1
  - If d(u,v) = 2: distance = 1 (added edge)
  - If d(u,v) ≥ 3: distance = 2 (shortest path is still through the tree, but we can't go directly)

Actually, in the new graph, there's an edge between u and v if they share a common neighbor in original tree. This forms a graph where the diameter is smaller.

But I think there's a simpler way: in the new graph, the distance between any two nodes is at most 2. More specifically:
- For any two nodes at distance 1 in original tree: distance 1
- For any two nodes at distance 2 in original tree: distance 1 (due to edge added)
- For any two nodes at distance ≥ 3 in original tree: distance 2 (they can reach each other in 2 steps via the tree)

Wait, that's not true. In the path 1-2-3-4, distance between 1 and 4 is 3 originally. In new graph:
- Add edge between 1 and 3 (share neighbor 2)
- Add edge between 2 and 4 (share neighbor 3)
- Distance between 1 and 4: 1→3→4 or 1→2→4, both length 2

So yes, in the new graph, all pairs have distance at most 2.

Therefore, the sum of distances is:
- For pairs with distance 1: contributes 1 each
- For pairs with distance 2: contributes 2 each

So I need to count:
- Number of pairs at original distance 1
- Number of pairs at original distance 2
- Number of pairs at original distance ≥ 3

But in the new graph:
- All pairs at original distance ≤ 2 have distance 1
- All pairs at original distance ≥ 3 have distance 2

So sum = (pairs at dist ≤ 2) × 1 + (pairs at dist ≥ 3) × 2

Wait, let me verify with examples:

**Example 1**: Star graph
- All pairs have distance ≤ 2, so all pairs have distance 1 in new graph
- Pairs: 6 pairs, sum = 6 ✓

**Example 2**: Path 1-2-3-4  
- Pairs at dist ≤ 2: 
  - dist 1: (1,2), (2,3), (3,4) → 3 pairs
  - dist 2: (1,3), (2,4) → 2 pairs  
  - Total: 5 pairs with distance 1
- Pairs at dist ≥ 3: (1,4) → 1 pair with distance 2
- Sum = 5×1 + 1×2 = 7 ✓

Perfect!

## Algorithm

1. Build the tree from input
2. For each pair of nodes, compute their distance in original tree using BFS/DFS
3. Count pairs with distance 1, 2, and ≥ 3
4. Sum = (pairs with dist 1 or 2) × 1 + (pairs with dist ≥ 3) × 2

Since n ≤ 200,000, we can't compute all pairs of distances naively (O(n²)).

We need a more efficient approach.

Better approach:
- For each node, count the number of nodes at distance 1 and 2
- Use tree DP to efficiently compute distances

Actually, let me reconsider. Since the final answer depends only on the original distances, I can use the fact that:
- For each node, its neighbors are at distance 1
- For each node, its neighbors of neighbors are at distance 2 (excluding itself)

But I need to avoid double counting.

I can use a BFS from each node to compute distances, but that would be O(n²). 

Alternative: Use tree diameter and centroid properties? 

Actually, with n ≤ 200,000, we need an O(n) or O(n log n) solution.

I can do:
1. Build adjacency list
2. For each node u, use BFS to compute distances to all other nodes
3. Keep track of distance counts

But this is O(n²) which might be too slow for n=200,000.

Wait, let me check constraints: n ≤ 200,000. O(n²) would be 40e9 operations, which is too slow.

I need a more efficient way to count pairs at distance 1 and 2.

I can:
1. Count degree of each node (pairs at distance 1)
2. For pairs at distance 2: for each node, the number of pairs among its neighbors of its neighbors that don't already have a direct edge? 

But pairs at distance 2 are counted once.

Actually, I can:
- Count total pairs at distance ≤ 2: this includes all pairs of nodes that share a common neighbor or are adjacent
- Count pairs at distance ≥ 3

But pairs at distance ≥ 3 are easy to count as total pairs minus pairs at distance ≤ 2.

Total pairs: n*(n-1)/2

Pairs at distance ≤ 2: 
- Pairs at distance 1: sum of degrees / 2
- Pairs at distance 2: need to count

Pairs at distance 2: for each node u, for each pair of neighbors v,w of u where v ≠ w and v,w ≠ u, the pair (v,w) is at distance 2.

But this overcounts? No, each pair (v,w) that shares neighbor u is counted once for u.

Each pair at distance 2 is counted exactly once (in the unique path of length 2, they share exactly one common neighbor).

So total pairs at distance 2 = sum over all nodes u of C(deg(u), 2) / 2? No.

Actually, for each node u, the number of pairs of neighbors is C(deg(u), 2). Each such pair is at distance 2 in the original tree.

So total pairs at distance 2 = sum over u of C(deg(u), 2)

And pairs at distance 1 = sum over u of deg(u) / 2

Total pairs at distance ≤ 2 = pairs at distance 1 + pairs at distance 2

Pairs at distance ≥ 3 = total pairs - pairs at distance ≤ 2

Sum of distances in new graph = (pairs at dist ≤ 2) × 1 + (pairs at dist ≥ 3) × 2

Let me verify with examples:

**Example 1**: Star with center 1, degree 3, others degree 1
- Pairs at dist 1: 3/2 = 1.5? No, must be integer.

Actually: pairs at dist 1 = sum(deg(u))/2 = (3+1+1+1)/2 = 6/2 = 3
- Pairs at dist 2: sum(C(deg(u),2)) = C(3,2) + 3×C(1,2) = 3 + 0 = 3  
- Pairs at dist ≤ 2: 3 + 3 = 6
- Pairs at dist ≥ 3: 6 - 6 = 0
- Sum = 6×1 + 0×2 = 6 ✓

**Example 2**: Path 1-2-3-4
- Degrees: 1,2,2,1
- Pairs at dist 1: (1+2+2+1)/2 = 6/2 = 3
- Pairs at dist 2: C(1,2) + C(2,2) + C(2,2) + C(1,2) = 0 + 1 + 1 + 0 = 2  
- Pairs at dist ≤ 2: 3 + 2 = 5
- Pairs at dist ≥ 3: 6 - 5 = 1
- Sum = 5×1 + 1×2 = 7 ✓

Perfect!

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Calculate degrees
    deg = [0] * (n + 1)
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        deg[i] = len(adj[i])
    
    # Pairs at distance 1: sum of degrees / 2
    pairs_dist_1 = sum(deg[1:]) // 2
    
    # Pairs at distance 2: sum of C(deg[i], 2) for all i
    pairs_dist_2 = 0
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        d = deg[i]
        pairs_dist_2 += d * (d - 1) // 2
    
    # Total pairs at distance <= 2
    pairs_le_2 = pairs_dist_1 + pairs_dist_2
    
    # Total pairs
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # Pairs at distance >= 3
    pairs_ge_3 = total_pairs - pairs_le_2
    
    # Sum of distances in new graph
    result = pairs_le_2 * 1 + pairs_ge_3 * 2
    print(result)
```

Wait, let me check if this handles all cases correctly. For n=2, the input gives one edge, so pairs at dist 1: 1, pairs at dist 2: 0, pairs_ge_3: 0, sum = 1, which is correct.

The algorithm is O(n), which should work for n ≤ 200,000.

View Problem Statement 
You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Sergey Semyonovich is a mayor of a county city N and he used to spend his days and nights in thoughts of further improvements of Nkers' lives. Unfortunately for him, anything and everything has been done already, and there are no more possible improvements he can think of during the day (he now prefers to sleep at night). However, his assistants have found a solution and they now draw an imaginary city on a paper sheet and suggest the mayor can propose its improvements.

Right now he has a map of some imaginary city with $n$ subway stations. Some stations are directly connected with tunnels in such a way that the whole map is a tree (assistants were short on time and enthusiasm). It means that there exists exactly one simple path between each pair of station. We call a path simple if it uses each tunnel no more than once.

One of Sergey Semyonovich's favorite quality objectives is the sum of all pairwise distances between every pair of stations. The distance between two stations is the minimum possible number of tunnels on a path between them.

Sergey Semyonovich decided to add new tunnels to the subway map. In particular, he connected any two stations $u$ and $v$ that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor, i.e. there exists such a station $w$ that the original map has a tunnel between $u$ and $w$ and a tunnel between $w$ and $v$. You are given a task to compute the sum of pairwise distances between all pairs of stations in the new map.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 200\,000$) — the number of subway stations in the imaginary city drawn by mayor's assistants. Each of the following $n - 1$ lines contains two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n$, $u_i \ne v_i$), meaning the station with these indices are connected with a direct tunnel.

It is guaranteed that these $n$ stations and $n - 1$ tunnels form a tree.


-----Output-----

Print one integer that is equal to the sum of distances between all pairs of stations after Sergey Semyonovich draws new tunnels between all pairs of stations that share a common neighbor in the original map.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
1 2
1 3
1 4

Output
6

Input
4
1 2
2 3
3 4

Output
7



-----Note-----

In the first sample, in the new map all pairs of stations share a direct connection, so the sum of distances is $6$.

In the second sample, the new map has a direct tunnel between all pairs of stations except for the pair $(1, 4)$. For these two stations the distance is $2$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). - Distance between u and v is 1 if either:
  1. They are adjacent in the original tree (direct edge), OR

- Distance between u and v is 2 otherwise (which only happens when they are not adjacent and don't share a common neighbor)

1. Build the tree from input
2. For each pair (u,v), determine if they are adjacent or share a common neighbor
3. Sum up the distances

But with n up to 200,000, O(n^2) is too slow.

I need to optimize.

Let me count:
- Number of pairs with distance 1 = number of edges in original tree + number of pairs that share a common neighbor
- Number of pairs with distance 2 = total pairs - pairs with distance 1

In a tree with n nodes, number of pairs = n*(n-1)/2
Number of edges = n-1

Number of pairs that share a common neighbor: for each node w, if it has degree d, then number of pairs among its neighbors is C(d,2) = d*(d-1)/2

So pairs with distance 1 = (n-1) + sum over all w of C(deg(w), 2)
Pairs with distance 2 = n*(n-1)/2 - [(n-1) + sum over w of C(deg(w), 2)]

Therefore, total sum of distances = 1 * pairs_with_distance_1 + 2 * pairs_with_distance_2

Let me verify with examples:

Example 1: star with n=4, center 1 has degree 3, others degree 1
- Pairs with distance 1 = (n-1) + sum C(deg(w),2) = 3 + C(3,2) = 3 + 3 = 6
- Pairs with distance 2 = 6 - 6 = 0
- Sum = 1*6 + 2*0 = 6 ✓

Example 2: path 1-2-3-4
- Degrees: deg(1)=1, deg(2)=2, deg(3)=2, deg(4)=1
- C(1,2)=0, C(2,2)=1, C(2,2)=1, C(1,2)=0
- Sum C(deg(w),2) = 0+1+1+0 = 2
- Pairs with distance 1 = (4-1) + 2 = 3 + 2 = 5
- Total pairs = 4*3/2 = 6
- Pairs with distance 2 = 6 - 5 = 1
- Sum = 1*5 + 2*1 = 7 ✓

Perfect!

## Solution

```python
n = int(input())
if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Calculate sum of C(deg(w), 2) for all nodes w
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    sum_c_deg = 0
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        d = len(adj[i])
        sum_c_deg += d * (d - 1) // 2
    
    # Pairs with distance 1: original edges + pairs sharing common neighbor
    pairs_dist_1 = (n - 1) + sum_c_deg
    
    # Pairs with distance 2
    pairs_dist_2 = total_pairs - pairs_dist_1
    
    # Total sum of distances
    result = pairs_dist_1 * 1 + pairs_dist_2 * 2
    print(result)
```

n = int(input())
if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n+1)]
    for _ in range(n-1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Calculate sum of C(deg(w), 2) for all nodes w
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    sum_c_deg = 0
    for i in range(1, n+1):
        d = len(adj[i])
        sum_c_deg += d * (d - 1) // 2
    
    # Pairs with distance 1: original edges + pairs sharing common neighbor
    pairs_dist_1 = (n - 1) + sum_c_deg
    
    # Pairs with distance 2
    pairs_dist_2 = total_pairs - pairs_dist_1
    
    # Total sum of distances
    result = pairs_dist_1 * 1 + pairs_dist_2 * 2
    print(result)

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    if n == 1:
        print(0)
        return
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(n - 1):

        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)

    # In the new graph, any two nodes that share a common neighbor will have distance 1

    # Distance between two nodes:
    # - 1 if they share a common neighbor in original tree

    # But I need to compute it properly

    direct_connections = set()
    
    # For each node, add edges between all pairs of its neighbors
    for node in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[node]

            for j in range(i + 1, len(neighbors)):
                u, v = neighbors[i], neighbors[j]
                # Add edge between u and v (distance 1 in new graph)
                direct_connections.add((min(u, v), max(u, v)))

    total_distance = 0

                total_distance += 1
```

```python
from collections import defaultdict

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    
    if n == 2:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Find all pairs that share a common neighbor and weren't already connected
    direct_connections = set()
    
    # For each node, find all pairs of its neighbors
    for node in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[node]
        # Create all pairs of neighbors
        for i in range(len(neighbors)):
            for j in range(i + 1, len(neighbors)):
                u, v = neighbors[i], neighbors[j]
                # If u and v already have an edge, skip (they're already connected)
                if u == v or v not in adj[u]:
                    # In original tree, u and v might not be directly connected
                    # But if they share a common neighbor (node) and don't already have edge, add connection
                    # We need to check if u and v are already connected
                    # In the original tree, two nodes are adjacent only if they are consecutive
                    # But we check via adj[u]
                    if v not in adj[u]:
                        direct_connections.add((min(u, v), max(u, v)))
    
    # Now compute sum of distances in new graph
    total_distance = 0
    
    # For each pair, the distance is:
    # - 1 if they have a direct connection in new graph
    # - 2 otherwise (since any two nodes that don't share a common neighbor will have path of length 2)
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        for j in range(i + 1, n + 1):
            if (i, j) in direct_connections:
                total_distance += 1
            else:
                total_distance += 2
    
    print(total_distance)

solve()
```

Wait, I see the issue. In the original tree, two nodes are directly connected if they are adjacent. So when we check if u and v already have an edge, we just check if v in adj[u].

But in the pair (u,v) in the application, we're adding edges between non-adjacent pairs that share a common neighbor.

```python
from collections import defaultdict

n = int(input())

if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Find all pairs that share a common neighbor and weren't already connected
    direct_connections = set()
    
    # For each node, find all pairs of its neighbors
    for node in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = set(adj[node])
        # For each pair of neighbors
        for u in neighbors:
            for v in neighbors:
                if u < v and v not in adj[u]:  # Make sure we don't duplicate and u,v are not already connected
                    direct_connections.add((u, v))
    
    # Compute sum of distances
    total_distance = 0
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        for j in range(i + 1, n + 1):
            if (i, j) in direct_connections:
                total_distance += 1
            else:
                total_distance += 2
    
    print(total_distance)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n nodes (subway stations)
2. We add new edges between any two nodes that share a common neighbor in the original tree
3. We need to find the sum of all pairwise distances in the new graph

## Key Insights

1. In the original tree, two nodes u and v share a common neighbor w if and only if w is adjacent to both u and v.

2. After adding these edges, we need to understand what the new graph looks like:
   - For any two nodes that have a common neighbor, they become directly connected
   - This means that if two nodes share a common neighbor, they will have distance 1 in the new graph

3. The important observation: In the new graph, two nodes are connected with distance 1 if they share a common neighbor, otherwise we need to find the shortest path.

4. Actually, let me think more deeply: if two nodes share a common neighbor, we add an edge between them. What happens to the tree structure?

5. After adding all such edges, we get a graph where every node is connected to all other nodes that have a common neighbor. 

6. Crucially, if we consider the complement: two nodes u and v might not share a common neighbor, but in the new graph, their distance might be reduced.

## Step-by-step Analysis

Let me trace through the examples:

**Example 1**: Star graph with center 1 connected to 2,3,4
- Original: 1-2, 1-3, 1-4
- For any two nodes (2,3), they share neighbor 1, so we add edge 2-3
- Similarly, add edges 2-4, 3-4
- Now we have a complete graph K4
- Distance between any pair is 1
- Total sum: 6 (since 4 choose 2 = 6 pairs)

**Example 2**: Path 1-2-3-4
- Original edges: 1-2, 2-3, 3-4
- Check pairs with common neighbor:
  - 1 and 3: share neighbor 2 ✓ → add edge 1-3
  - 1 and 4: no common neighbor (1 only connected to 2, 4 only to 3) ✗
  - 2 and 4: share neighbor 3 ✓ → add edge 2-4  
  - 1 and 2: already connected
  - 2 and 3: already connected
  - 3 and 4: already connected
- New graph has edges: 1-2, 1-3, 2-3, 2-4, 3-4
- Now we have a complete graph except missing edge 1-4
- Distance 1-4: path 1-2-4 or 1-3-4, so distance 2
- All other pairs have distance 1
- Total sum: (4 pairs with distance 1) + (1 pair with distance 2) = 4 + 2 = 6? Wait, that's not 7.

Let me recount:
- 1-2: distance 1
- 1-3: distance 1  
- 1-4: distance 2
- 2-3: distance 1
- 2-4: distance 1
- 3-4: distance 1

Sum = 1+1+2+1+1+1 = 7 ✓

## Solution Approach

The key insight: After adding edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, we get a graph where:

1. Two nodes u and v have distance 1 if they share a common neighbor in the original tree
2. Two nodes that don't share a common neighbor will have distance at most 2 (since in a tree, any two nodes have a unique path, and if they don't share a neighbor, their path might go through a common node)

Wait, let me reconsider. Actually, if two nodes don't share a common neighbor, can they still be close?

In the path example, 1 and 4 don't share a common neighbor (1 only adjacent to 2, 4 only adjacent to 3), so they don't get a direct edge. Their distance in the original tree is 3, but in the new graph it's 2 (via 1-2-4 or 1-3-4).

So the distance between any two nodes in the new graph is:
- 1 if they share a common neighbor
- 2 otherwise (because in a tree, the path between any two nodes goes through some node, and if they don't share a neighbor, the path can be shortened to length 2)

Actually, let me verify: if two nodes don't share a common neighbor, is their distance always 2 in the new graph?

Yes! Because in the new graph, we've added edges for all pairs that share a common neighbor. For any two nodes that don't share a common neighbor, they might still have distance 2 because they can go through a common path.

But is there a case where distance > 2? I don't think so - in the new graph, we've essentially created a graph where most pairs are close.

## Algorithm

1. Build the original tree
2. For every pair of nodes that share a common neighbor, they now have distance 1
3. For all other pairs, they have distance 2
4. Sum the distances

Wait, let me verify this with the examples:
- Example 1: All pairs share a common neighbor (they all share center 1), so all distances are 1. Sum = 6. ✓
- Example 2: Pairs that share common neighbor: (1,3), (2,4). So pairs with distance 1: (1,2), (1,3), (2,3), (2,4), (3,4) → 5 pairs. Pair (1,4) has distance 2. Sum = 5*1 + 1*2 = 7. ✓

The algorithm:
1. Count how many pairs share a common neighbor
2. Total pairs = n*(n-1)/2
3. Pairs with distance 1 = count of pairs that share a common neighbor
4. Pairs with distance 2 = total pairs - pairs with distance 1
5. Answer = (pairs with distance 1) * 1 + (pairs with distance 2) * 2

But is it true that all non-sharing-pair have distance 2?

Actually, I think yes. In the new graph, we've added edges between nodes sharing common neighbors. For any two nodes that don't share a common neighbor, their shortest path in the original tree might be longer, but in the new graph, they can go through intermediate nodes. The key is that the minimum distance between any two non-neighbors in the new graph is 2.

Therefore, the answer is:
- Count all pairs (u,v) such that u and v share a common neighbor w in original tree
- For each such pair, distance becomes 1
- For all other pairs, distance becomes 2

## Implementation

```python
n = int(input())
if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Count pairs that share a common neighbor
    common_neighbor_pairs = 0
    
    # For each node, count pairs of its neighbors
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[i]
        if len(neighbors) >= 2:
            # Number of ways to choose 2 neighbors from this node
            num_neighbors = len(neighbors)
            common_neighbor_pairs += num_neighbors * (num_neighbors - 1) // 2
    
    # Total pairs
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # Pairs with distance 1
    distance_1_pairs = common_neighbor_pairs
    
    # Pairs with distance 2
    distance_2_pairs = total_pairs - distance_1_pairs
    
    # Sum of distances
    result = distance_1_pairs * 1 + distance_2_pairs * 2
    print(result)
```

View Extracted Code 
n = int(input())
if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Count pairs that share a common neighbor
    common_neighbor_pairs = 0
    
    # For each node, count pairs of its neighbors
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[i]
        if len(neighbors) >= 2:
            # Number of ways to choose 2 neighbors from this node
            num_neighbors = len(neighbors)
            common_neighbor_pairs += num_neighbors * (num_neighbors - 1) // 2
    
    # Total pairs
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # Pairs with distance 1
    distance_1_pairs = common_neighbor_pairs
    
    # Pairs with distance 2
    distance_2_pairs = total_pairs - distance_1_pairs
    
    # Sum of distances
    result = distance_1_pairs * 1 + distance_2_pairs * 2
    print(result)

You are an expert Python programmer. You will be given a question (problem specification) and will generate a correct Python program that matches the specification and passes all tests.

Sergey Semyonovich is a mayor of a county city N and he used to spend his days and nights in thoughts of further improvements of Nkers' lives. Unfortunately for him, anything and everything has been done already, and there are no more possible improvements he can think of during the day (he now prefers to sleep at night). However, his assistants have found a solution and they now draw an imaginary city on a paper sheet and suggest the mayor can propose its improvements.

Right now he has a map of some imaginary city with $n$ subway stations. Some stations are directly connected with tunnels in such a way that the whole map is a tree (assistants were short on time and enthusiasm). It means that there exists exactly one simple path between each pair of station. We call a path simple if it uses each tunnel no more than once.

One of Sergey Semyonovich's favorite quality objectives is the sum of all pairwise distances between every pair of stations. The distance between two stations is the minimum possible number of tunnels on a path between them.

Sergey Semyonovich decided to add new tunnels to the subway map. In particular, he connected any two stations $u$ and $v$ that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor, i.e. there exists such a station $w$ that the original map has a tunnel between $u$ and $w$ and a tunnel between $w$ and $v$. You are given a task to compute the sum of pairwise distances between all pairs of stations in the new map.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 200\,000$) — the number of subway stations in the imaginary city drawn by mayor's assistants. Each of the following $n - 1$ lines contains two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n$, $u_i \ne v_i$), meaning the station with these indices are connected with a direct tunnel.

It is guaranteed that these $n$ stations and $n - 1$ tunnels form a tree.


-----Output-----

Print one integer that is equal to the sum of distances between all pairs of stations after Sergey Semyonovich draws new tunnels between all pairs of stations that share a common neighbor in the original map.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
1 2
1 3
1 4

Output
6

4
1 2
2 3
3 4

Output
7

In the second sample, the new map has a direct tunnel between all pairs of stations except for the pair $(1, 4)$. View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n nodes (subway stations)
2. We add new edges between any two stations that share a common neighbor in the original tree
3. We need to find the sum of all pairwise distances in the resulting graph

## Key Insights

1. In the original tree, any two nodes that share a common neighbor will have a direct edge added
2. After adding these edges, the resulting graph might have certain properties
3. Let me analyze the examples:

**Example 1**: Star graph (1 connected to 2, 3, 4)
- Original: 1-2, 1-3, 1-4
- After adding edges: 2,3,4 all share neighbor 1, so we add edges (2,3), (2,4), (3,4)
- Result: Complete graph K4 with distances = 1 for all pairs
- Sum = 6 (since 4 choose 2 = 6 pairs, each with distance 1)

**Example 2**: Path graph (1-2-3-4)
- Original: 1-2, 2-3, 3-4
- Nodes sharing neighbors:
  - 1 and 3: share neighbor 2
  - 2 and 4: share neighbor 3
  - 1 and 2: adjacent (already connected)
  - 2 and 3: adjacent (already connected)
  - 3 and 4: adjacent (already connected)
- So we add edges (1,3) and (2,4)
- Final graph has edges: 1-2, 2-3, 3-4, 1-3, 2-4
- Distance(1,4) = 2 (path 1-3-4 or 1-2-4)
- All other distances are 1
- Sum = 1+1+1+1+1+2 = 7

## General Approach

The key insight is that after adding edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, we get a graph where:
- All nodes at the same "level" (distance from some root) might become connected
- The resulting graph has diameter at most 2 in many cases

Actually, let me think more systematically:

After adding edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, the resulting graph has a special property: for any two nodes, if they don't have a direct edge, their distance is at most 2.

For each pair of nodes (i,j):
- If they are directly connected (original or added), distance = 1
- If they are not directly connected, distance = 2 (since they must have a common neighbor in the original tree)

Wait, is that always true? Let me verify.

In the original tree, if two nodes share a common neighbor w, then after adding the edge (u,v), we have a direct edge. But more importantly, if two nodes don't share a common neighbor, their distance might be greater than 2 in the original tree, but after adding edges, it could reduce.

Actually, let me reconsider. The new graph will have:
- All original edges
- All edges between nodes that share a common neighbor in the original tree

This means that for any two nodes that have a common neighbor in the original tree, they get a direct edge.

The key insight: Two nodes have distance 1 if they are adjacent (original or added), otherwise distance 2.

Is that always true? Actually, no - if two nodes are at distance 2 in the original tree, they might not share a common neighbor, so they remain at distance 2.

Wait, let me think about the structure:

In a tree, two nodes share a common neighbor if and only if they have a common neighbor in the original tree. 

After adding these edges, the maximum distance between any two nodes becomes at most 2. 

So for any pair (i,j):
- If i and j are adjacent in the new graph → distance = 1  
- Otherwise → distance = 2

Therefore, the sum of pairwise distances = (number of pairs with distance 1) + 2 × (number of pairs with distance 2)

Let me calculate:
- Total pairs = n × (n-1) / 2
- Pairs with distance 1 = (original edges) + (new edges added)
- Pairs with distance 2 = total pairs - pairs with distance 1

In the new graph, pairs with distance 1 include:
- All original edges (n-1)
- All pairs that share a common neighbor in the original tree

For each node w, the number of pairs among its neighbors that share w as a common neighbor is C(degree(w), 2).

So total new edges = Σ C(degree(w), 2) for all w.

Therefore:
- Number of pairs with distance 1 = (n-1) + Σ C(degree(w), 2)
- Number of pairs with distance 2 = n(n-1)/2 - [(n-1) + Σ C(degree(w), 2)]

Final answer = (n-1) + Σ C(degree(w), 2) + 2 × [n(n-1)/2 - (n-1) - Σ C(degree(w), 2)]

Simplifying:
= (n-1) + Σ C(degree(w), 2) + n(n-1) - 2(n-1) - 2 × Σ C(degree(w), 2)
= n(n-1) - (n-1) - Σ C(degree(w), 2)
= (n-1)(n-1) - Σ C(degree(w), 2)
= (n-1)² - Σ C(degree(w), 2)

Wait, let me recalculate more carefully:

Sum of distances = (number of pairs with distance 1) + 2 × (number of pairs with distance 2)
= [(n-1) + Σ C(degree(w), 2)] + 2 × [n(n-1)/2 - (n-1) - Σ C(degree(w), 2)]
= (n-1) + Σ C(degree(w), 2) + n(n-1) - 2(n-1) - 2 × Σ C(degree(w), 2)
= n(n-1) - (n-1) - Σ C(degree(w), 2)
= (n-1)(n-1) - Σ C(degree(w), 2)
= (n-1)² - Σ C(degree(w), 2)

But let me verify with examples:

**Example 1**: n=4, star graph
- Degrees: [3,1,1,1]
- Σ C(degree(w), 2) = C(3,2) + C(1,2) + C(1,2) + C(1,2) = 3 + 0 + 0 + 0 = 3
- Answer = (4-1)² - 3 = 9 - 3 = 6 ✓

**Example 2**: n=4, path 1-2-3-4
- Degrees: [1,2,2,1]
- Σ C(degree(w), 2) = C(1,2) + C(2,2) + C(2,2) + C(1,2) = 0 + 1 + 1 + 0 = 2
- Answer = (4-1)² - 2 = 9 - 2 = 7 ✓

Perfect! The formula works.

## Solution

```python
n = int(input())
if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Read edges and calculate degrees
    degree = [0] * (n + 1)
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        degree[u] += 1
        degree[v] += 1
    
    # Calculate sum of C(degree[i], 2) for all nodes
    sum_c_pairs = 0
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        d = degree[i]
        if d >= 2:
            sum_c_pairs += d * (d - 1) // 2
    
    # Answer = (n-1)^2 - sum_c_pairs
    result = (n - 1) * (n - 1) - sum_c_pairs
    print(result)
```

View Full Response 
I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n nodes (subway stations)
2. We add new edges between any two nodes that share a common neighbor in the original tree
3. We need to find the sum of pairwise distances in the new graph

## Key Insights

1. In the original tree, two nodes share a common neighbor if they are both connected to the same node.
2. After adding edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, we create a new graph.
3. The key observation is that nodes that share a common neighbor will now be directly connected.

## Analysis of the Examples

**Example 1:** Star graph with center 1 connected to 2, 3, 4
- Original: 1-2, 1-3, 1-4
- Nodes 2,3,4 all share neighbor 1
- After adding edges: We add edges between (2,3), (2,4), (3,4)
- Result: Complete graph K4 with 4 nodes
- Sum of pairwise distances: 6 (each pair has distance 1, there are C(4,2)=6 pairs)

**Example 2:** Path graph 1-2-3-4
- Original: 1-2, 2-3, 3-4
- Nodes sharing common neighbors:
  - 1 and 3: both connected to 2
  - 2 and 4: both connected to 3
- After adding edges: Add (1,3) and (2,4)
- Result: We have edges: 1-2, 2-3, 3-4, 1-3, 2-4
- Distance between 1 and 4: path 1-2-4 (distance 2)
- Sum of distances: 1+1+1+1+1+2 = 7

## Algorithm Approach

After adding edges between nodes that share common neighbors, we need to compute the sum of pairwise distances.

The key insight is that the new graph will be a "clique" for each neighbor group. Nodes that share a common neighbor will now be directly connected.

For any node w in the original tree, all nodes connected to w will form a clique in the new graph.

The sum of distances in the new graph can be calculated by:
1. For each node w in the tree, consider the group of nodes adjacent to w
2. Within each such group, all pairs have distance 1 (since they're directly connected)
3. Between different groups, we need to calculate the actual distances

However, there's a more elegant approach:

**Key Insight:** After adding all edges between nodes sharing a common neighbor, the resulting graph will have the property that for any two nodes u and v:
- If u and v share a common neighbor in the original tree, their distance is 1
- Otherwise, their distance is at most 2 (since they might be distance 2 through some path)

Actually, let me think more systematically:

After adding edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, the graph becomes such that:
- Any two nodes that are at distance ≤ 2 in the original tree will have distance ≤ 2 in the new graph
- But specifically, if they share a common neighbor, they become distance 1
- If they don't share a common neighbor, they might still be distance 2 or more

Wait, let me reconsider with the examples:

In the path 1-2-3-4:
- 1 and 3 share neighbor 2 → distance becomes 1
- 2 and 4 share neighbor 3 → distance becomes 1  
- 1 and 4 don't share a common neighbor → distance is 2

So the sum is: 
- (1,2): 1
- (1,3): 1  
- (1,4): 2
- (2,3): 1
- (2,4): 1
- (3,4): 1

Total = 7 ✓

## Solution Strategy

1. Build the original tree
2. For each node, identify its neighbors
3. Find all pairs of nodes that share a common neighbor
4. The distance between any two nodes that share a common neighbor is now 1
5. For pairs that don't share a common neighbor, their distance is the minimum of:
   - The original distance (in the tree)
   - 2 (since we can go through a common neighbor)

But actually, in the new graph, the distance between any two nodes that don't share a direct connection might be 2.

## Implementation

```python
from collections import defaultdict, deque

def solve():
    n = int(input())
    
    if n == 2:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Find all pairs that share a common neighbor
    # We'll calculate the distance between each pair in the new graph
    # Two nodes share a common neighbor if they both are adjacent to some node w
    
    # First, create a set of pairs that will have direct edges (distance 1)
    direct_edges = set()
    
    # For each node, collect its neighbors
    for w in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[w]
        # All pairs among neighbors of w will have direct edge
        for i in range(len(neighbors)):
            for j in range(i + 1, len(neighbors)):
                u, v = neighbors[i], neighbors[j]
                # Add undirected edge
                direct_edges.add((min(u, v), max(u, v)))
    
    # Calculate sum of distances in new graph
    total_distance = 0
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        for j in range(i + 1, n + 1):
            # If there's a direct edge in the new graph, distance is 1
            if (i, j) in direct_edges:
                total_distance += 1
            else:
                # Otherwise, the distance is at most 2
                # We need to find the shortest path in the new graph
                # Since the original graph is a tree, and we've added edges,
                # the distance between any two non-adjacent nodes will be at most 2
                # Actually, it might be more complex...
                
                # Let's find the distance using BFS in the new graph
                # But we can be smarter: in the new graph, two nodes are at distance 2
                # if they have no common neighbor and are not directly connected
                
                # Actually, let me reconsider: if two nodes don't share a common neighbor,
                # they might still be connected through a path of length 2
                
                # But in the new graph, the distance is:
                # 1 if they share a common neighbor
                # 2 if they don't share a common neighbor and are in the same "component" 
                
                # If they don't share a common neighbor, what's the distance?
                # We need to check if there's a path of length 2
                
                # Actually, let's think: in the new graph, we only added edges between nodes
                # that share a common neighbor. So if two nodes don't share a common neighbor,
                # they might still be connected through a path of length 2 if they have a common 
                # neighbor in some way.
                
                # But no, we only added edges between nodes that share a common neighbor.
                
                # Actually, the distance in the new graph for non-direct pairs is 2
                # because: if a, b don't share a common neighbor, but a connects to c and b connects to d,
                # and c and d might be connected through something? Not necessarily.
                
                # Let me trace the example: in 1-2-3-4, 1 and 4 don't share a common neighbor
                # Distance is 2 (1-2-4)
                
                # So in general, for any two nodes that don't share a common neighbor,
                # their distance in the new graph might be 2.
                
                # But is this always true?
                # Yes, because in a tree, any two nodes have a unique path.
                # If they don't share a common neighbor, they will have a path of length 2
                # through some intermediate point, and since we've connected all nodes
                # that share a common neighbor, this path exists.
                
                # Actually, no - we haven't added edges between non-sharing nodes.
                
                # Let me reconsider: the new graph contains the original tree edges
                # plus edges between nodes that share a common neighbor.
                
                # So two nodes at distance d in the original tree might have distance less than d
                # in the new graph if they share a common neighbor.
                
                # For non-sharing nodes, what's the distance?
                
                # It's actually the length of the shortest path in the new graph.
                
                # Since we have the original tree edges, and we've added edges between nodes
                # sharing a common neighbor, the distance between any two nodes is at most 2.
                
                # Why? Because in the original tree, they have a path of some length.
                # But in the new graph, we might have shortened it through intermediate nodes.
                
                # However, if two nodes don't share a common neighbor, the distance might still be 2
                # because they might have a path through a common neighbor?
                
                # No - if they don't share a common neighbor, they don't get a direct connection.
                
                # But they might still have a path of length 2 in the new graph.
                
                # Actually, in the new graph, there might be multiple paths, but since we only added
                # edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, nodes that don't share a common
                # neighbor won't have direct edges.
                
                # However, they might still be connected through other paths.
                
                # This is getting complex. Let me implement a simpler approach.
                
                # Actually, I recall that in such problems, the final distance between any two
                # non-adjacent nodes that don't share a common neighbor is 2.
                
                # Let me verify with examples:
                # Example 1: all pairs share common neighbor → all distance 1
                # Example 2: 1 and 4 don't share common neighbor → distance 2
                # All other pairs share common neighbor → distance 1
                
                total_distance += 2
    
    print(total_distance)

solve()
```

Wait, I think I'm overcomplicating this. Let me reconsider.

Actually, in the new graph:
- Any two nodes that share a common neighbor have distance 1
- For any two nodes that don't share a common neighbor, their distance is 2 (since they might still be connected through a path of length 2)

But is every pair of nodes that doesn't share a common neighbor at distance 2?

Yes, because:
1. The original graph is a tree, so any two nodes have a path
2. In the new graph, we've added edges between nodes that share a common neighbor
3. If two nodes don't share a common neighbor, they still have the original tree path
4. But more importantly, in the new graph, the distance between them can't exceed 2

Actually, no - if two nodes are distance 3 apart and don't share a common neighbor, their distance might still be more than 2.

Let me think differently. 

**Key insight:** After adding edges between nodes that share a common neighbor, the resulting graph will have diameter at most 2. This is because:
- For any two nodes u and v, if they share a common neighbor, distance is 1
- If they don't share a common neighbor, their distance is at most 2 (they can go through a common neighbor)

But this isn't true - if u and v don't share a common neighbor, they might still be connected through a path of length 2 in the tree.

Actually, I think the correct answer is:
- Count pairs that share a common neighbor → distance 1
- Count pairs that don't share a common neighbor → distance 2

```python
from collections import defaultdict

n = int(input())

if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = defaultdict(list)
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Find all pairs that share a common neighbor
    shared_neighbor_pairs = set()
    
    for w in range(1, n + 1):
        neighbors = adj[w]
        for i in range(len(neighbors)):
            for j in range(i + 1, len(neighbors)):
                u, v = neighbors[i], neighbors[j]
                shared_neighbor_pairs.add((min(u, v), max(u, v)))
    
    # Calculate sum of distances
    total_distance = 0
    
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        for j in range(i + 1, n + 1):
            if (i, j) in shared_neighbor_pairs:
                total_distance += 1
            else:
                total_distance += 2
    
    print(total_distance)
```
