It means that there exists exactly one simple path between each pair of station. We call a path simple if it uses each tunnel no more than once.

One of Sergey Semyonovich's favorite quality objectives is the sum of all pairwise distances between every pair of stations. The distance between two stations is the minimum possible number of tunnels on a path between them.

Sergey Semyonovich decided to add new tunnels to the subway map. In particular, he connected any two stations $u$ and $v$ that were not connected with a direct tunnel but share a common neighbor, i.e. there exists such a station $w$ that the original map has a tunnel between $u$ and $w$ and a tunnel between $w$ and $v$. You are given a task to compute the sum of pairwise distances between all pairs of stations in the new map.


-----Input-----

The first line of the input contains a single integer $n$ ($2 \leq n \leq 200\,000$) — the number of subway stations in the imaginary city drawn by mayor's assistants. Each of the following $n - 1$ lines contains two integers $u_i$ and $v_i$ ($1 \leq u_i, v_i \leq n$, $u_i \ne v_i$), meaning the station with these indices are connected with a direct tunnel.

It is guaranteed that these $n$ stations and $n - 1$ tunnels form a tree.


-----Output-----

Print one integer that is equal to the sum of distances between all pairs of stations after Sergey Semyonovich draws new tunnels between all pairs of stations that share a common neighbor in the original map.


-----Examples-----
Input
4
1 2
1 3
1 4

Output
6

Input
4
1 2
2 3
3 4

Output
7



-----Note-----

In the first sample, in the new map all pairs of stations share a direct connection, so the sum of distances is $6$.

In the second sample, the new map has a direct tunnel between all pairs of stations except for the pair $(1, 4)$. For these two stations the distance is $2$.### Format: Read the inputs from stdin solve the problem and write the answer to stdout (do not directly test on the sample inputs). Enclose your code within delimiters as follows. Ensure that when the python program runs, it reads the inputs, runs the algorithm and writes output to STDOUT.
```python
# YOUR CODE HERE
```

I'll solve this step by step.

## Problem Understanding

1. We have a tree with n nodes (subway stations) and n-1 edges (tunnels)
2. We add new tunnels between any two stations that share a common neighbor in the original tree
3. We need to find the sum of all pairwise distances in the new graph

## Key Insights

1. In the original tree, any two nodes that share a common neighbor will now have a direct edge
2. After adding these edges, what structure do we get?

Let me analyze the examples:

**Example 1**: Star graph (1 connected to 2,3,4)
- All pairs share a common neighbor (station 1)
- After adding edges, we get a complete graph K4
- Distance between any two nodes = 1
- Sum = 6 (since there are C(4,2) = 6 pairs)

**Example 2**: Path graph (1-2-3-4)
- Station 1 shares neighbor 2 with station 2
- Station 2 shares neighbor 1 with station 1, and neighbor 3 with station 3
- Station 3 shares neighbor 2 with station 2, and neighbor 4 with station 4
- So we add edges: (1,3), (2,4)
- Final graph: 1-2-3-4 with additional edges 1-3, 2-4
- Distance (1,4) = 2 (path 1-2-4 or 1-3-4)
- All other distances = 1
- Sum = 6×1 + 1×2 = 8? Wait, let me recount...

Actually, let me recalculate example 2:
- Pairs and distances:
  - (1,2): 1
  - (1,3): 1 (new edge)
  - (1,4): 2 (path 1-2-4 or 1-3-4)
  - (2,3): 1 
  - (2,4): 1 (new edge)
  - (3,4): 1
- Sum = 1+1+2+1+1+1 = 7 ✓

## General Solution Approach

1. First, identify which pairs get direct edges in the new graph
2. For each pair, calculate their distance in the new graph
3. Sum all distances

But we can optimize this by understanding the structure:

In the new graph, two nodes have a direct edge if they share a common neighbor in the original tree.

The key insight is that this creates a graph where:
- All nodes that are at distance ≤ 2 in the original tree will now have direct connections
- The distance between two nodes in the new graph is the minimum of:
  - Their original distance (if they already had a direct connection)
  - 1 (if they share a common neighbor)

Wait, actually the distance becomes 1 if they share a common neighbor, or their original distance otherwise.

But there's a better way: in the new graph, the maximum distance between any two nodes is 2.

**Special Cases:**
- If two nodes are connected directly in original tree: distance = 1 in new graph
- If two nodes are at distance 2 in original tree: they share a common neighbor → distance = 1 in new graph  
- If two nodes are at distance ≥ 3 in original tree: their distance in new graph is 2 (through some common neighbor)

Actually, let me reconsider: two nodes that are at distance ≥ 2 will always have a common neighbor if they are at distance 2 or more? No.

Two nodes at distance 3: they don't necessarily share a common neighbor.

But in the new graph, two nodes are connected directly if they share a common neighbor. So:

- Distance between two nodes in new graph = 
  - 1 if they share a common neighbor (which happens if their original distance ≤ 2)
  - Original distance if original distance ≥ 3? No.

Actually, if two nodes have original distance d:
- If d = 1: they are directly connected → distance = 1
- If d = 2: they share a common neighbor → distance = 1  
- If d ≥ 3: they don't share a common neighbor, so distance = d?

Wait, that's not right either. In a tree, if two nodes have distance ≥ 3, they might still have a common neighbor somewhere.

Let me think differently.

## Correct Approach

In the new graph:
- For any two nodes u, v, the distance is:
  - 1 if they share a common neighbor in the original tree
  - The original distance between u and v otherwise

Two nodes share a common neighbor in the original tree if and only if they are at distance exactly 2 in the original tree (since in a tree, any two nodes at distance 2 have exactly one common neighbor).

So:
- If dist(u,v) = 1: distance in new graph = 1
- If dist(u,v) = 2: distance in new graph = 1 (they share common neighbor)
- If dist(u,v) ≥ 3: distance in new graph = dist(u,v) (since they don't share common neighbor)

Therefore, we can solve this by:
1. Compute the original distance between all pairs
2. Sum 1 for pairs at distance 1 or 2
3. Sum original distance for pairs at distance ≥ 3

But computing all pairs distance is O(n²) which might be too slow.

Alternative approach:

We can compute the sum of distances in the original tree, then adjust for the new connections.

Wait, let me verify with examples:

**Example 1 (star)**: All pairs have original distance 1 or 2 (all pairs have distance 1 in original tree). In new graph, all distances = 1. So sum = 6.

**Example 2 (path 1-2-3-4)**:
- Original distances: (1,2):1, (1,3):2, (1,4):3, (2,3):1, (2,4):2, (3,4):1
- In new graph: (1,2):1, (1,3):1, (1,4):2, (2,3):1, (2,4):1, (3,4):1
- Sum = 1+1+2+1+1+1 = 7 ✓

This matches!

So the algorithm:
1. Build the tree
2. For each pair of nodes, if their original distance is 1 or 2, distance in new graph is 1
3. If original distance ≥ 3, distance in new graph is original distance
4. Sum all these distances

But O(n²) might be too slow for n=200,000.

We need to optimize.

## Optimized Approach

We can:
1. Compute the sum of original distances in the tree
2. For pairs at distance 1: they contribute 1 in new graph, same as original
3. For pairs at distance 2: they contribute 1 in new graph instead of 2, so we save 1 each
4. For pairs at distance ≥ 3: they contribute original distance in new graph, same as original

So: new_sum = original_sum - (number_of_pairs_at_distance_2)

This works because:
- Distance 1 pairs: difference = 0
- Distance 2 pairs: original distance = 2, new distance = 1, so we save 1
- Distance ≥ 3 pairs: same

Therefore:
new_sum = original_sum - (count of pairs with original distance 2)

Now we need to:
1. Compute original sum of all pairwise distances in a tree
2. Count how many pairs have distance exactly 2

## Computing sum of pairwise distances in a tree

We can use the fact that the sum of pairwise distances can be computed by considering each edge's contribution.

For each edge (u,v), if removing it splits the tree into components of size a and b, then this edge contributes (a * b) to the total sum of all pairwise distances.

## Counting pairs of distance exactly 2

For distance exactly 2: we need two nodes that are connected through a common neighbor.

In other words: pairs (u,v) such that there exists w where (u,w) and (w,v) are edges.

We can count this by iterating through each node w and counting the number of pairs among its neighbors.

For a node w with degree d, it contributes C(d,2) pairs that have distance 2.

Total pairs at distance 2 = Σ C(deg(w), 2) for all w.

## Implementation

```python
def solve():
    n = int(input())
    
    if n == 2:
        print(1)
        return
    
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Function to calculate sum of pairwise distances in a tree
    def tree_distance_sum():
        # For each edge, calculate contribution to total distance sum
        total = 0
        
        # Use DFS to compute subtree sizes
        visited = [False] * (n + 1)
        
        def dfs(u, parent):
            size = 1
            for v in adj[u]:
                if v != parent:
                    dfs(v, u)
                    size += size_of_subtree[v]
            size_of_subtree[u] = size
        
        size_of_subtree = [0] * (n + 1)
        
        # We'll do DFS from node 1
        dfs(1, -1)
        
        # Now, for each edge, calculate contribution
        # When we remove edge (u, parent), we get components of size size_of_subtree[u] and (n - size_of_subtree[u])
        # This edge contributes size_of_subtree[u] * (n - size_of_subtree[u]) to total sum
        for u in range(1, n + 1):
            for v in adj[u]:
                if v != u and (not (v < u)) or v == 1:  # To avoid double counting
                    # Actually, we need to compute contribution of each edge
                    pass
        
        # Better approach: do DFS to compute contribution of each edge
        contribution = 0
        visited = [False] * (n + 1)
        
        def dfs(u, parent):
            nonlocal contribution
            size = 1
            for v in adj[u]:
                if v != parent:
                    dfs(v, u)
                    size += size_of_subtree[v]
                    contribution += size_of_subtree[v] * (n - size_of_subtree[v])
            size_of_subtree[u] = size
        
        dfs(1, -1)
        return contribution
    
    # Actually, simpler approach: calculate contribution of each edge
    total_original = 0
    visited = [False] * (n + 1)
    
    def dfs(u, parent):
        size = 1
        for v in adj[u]:
            if v != parent:
                dfs(v, u)
                size += size_of_subtree[v]
                total_original += size_of_subtree[v] * (n - size_of_subtree[v])
        size_of_subtree[u] = size
    
    size_of_subtree = [0] * (n + 1)
    dfs(1, -1)
    
    # Count pairs at distance exactly 2
    pairs_distance_2 = 0
    for u in range(1, n + 1):
        deg = len(adj[u])
        pairs_distance_2 += deg * (deg - 1) // 2
    
    result = total_original - pairs_distance_2
    print(result)

solve()
```

In a tree, if two nodes have distance d, they have a unique path. If they don't share a common neighbor, then their distance in the new graph might be 2, not d.

- Pairs and new distances:

General rule:
- If original distance d ≤ 2: new distance = 1
- If original distance d ≥ 3: new distance = 2

In a general tree, for any two nodes at distance ≥ 3, do they always have new distance 2?

No. Consider a path 1-2-3-4-5. Pair (1,5) has distance 4. Do they share a common neighbor? 
- 1's neighbors: 2

- In new graph, distance between 1 and 5: could be 1-2-3-4-5 or 1-2-3-5 or 1-2-4-5 or 1-3-4-5 or 1-2-3-4-5.

But in the new graph, since 1-2, 2-3, 3-4, 4-5 are present, and we add edges between nodes that share common neighbors:
- 1 and 3: share common neighbor 2 → add edge

So yes, in this case distance is 2.

Actually, for any two nodes at distance d ≥ 3 in a tree, they will have distance 2 in the new graph because they will be connected through their common neighbors.

In the new graph, the diameter is at most 2.

Therefore:
- New distance = 1 if original distance ≤ 2
- New distance = 2 if original distance ≥ 3

So the sum in new graph = sum over all pairs:
- If dist(u,v) ≤ 2: contribute 1
- If dist(u,v) ≥ 3: contribute 2

Original sum = sum over all pairs: dist(u,v)

New sum = (number of pairs with dist(u,v) ≤ 2) * 1 + (number of pairs with dist(u,v) ≥ 3) * 2

= original_sum - (number of pairs with dist(u,v) ≥ 3) + (number of pairs with dist(u,v) ≥ 3)

Wait, let me rewrite:

New_sum = Σ_{d=1}^2 1 + Σ_{d=3}^∞ 2
= (total_pairs - pairs_with_d≥3) + 2 * pairs_with_d≥3
= total_pairs + pairs_with_d≥3

Where total_pairs = C(n,2)

And pairs_with_d≥3 = (original_sum - Σ_{d=1}^2 d * (count of pairs at distance d))

Let me define:
- pairs_d1 = number of pairs at distance 1
- pairs_d2 = number of pairs at distance 2  
- pairs_d3_plus = number of pairs at distance ≥ 3

Then:
- pairs_d1 + pairs_d2 + pairs_d3_plus = C(n,2)
- original_sum = 1*pairs_d1 + 2*pairs_d2 + Σ_{d=3}^∞ d * pairs_d

New_sum = 1*(pairs_d1 + pairs_d2) + 2*pairs_d3_plus
= (pairs_d1 + pairs_d2 + pairs_d3_plus) + pairs_d3_plus
= C(n,2) + pairs_d3_plus

So we need to compute the number of pairs with distance ≥ 3.

Number of pairs with distance ≥ 3 = C(n,2) - pairs_d1 - pairs_d2

So:
New_sum = C(n,2) + (C(n,2) - pairs_d1 - pairs_d2)
= 2*C(n,2) - pairs_d1 - pairs_d2

Now I need to compute:
- pairs_d1: number of edges in the tree = n-1
- pairs_d2: number of pairs of nodes that have distance exactly 2

pairs_d2 = Σ C(deg(v), 2) for all vertices v

So final answer = 2 * C(n,2) - (n-1) - Σ C(deg(v), 2)

Let me verify with examples:

**Example 1**: n=4
- C(4,2) = 6
- pairs_d1 = n-1 = 3
- pairs_d2 = C(3,2) = 3 (center has degree 3, C(3,2)=3)
- New_sum = 2*6 - 3 - 3 = 12 - 6 = 6 ✓

**Example 2**: n=4, path 1-2-3-4
- C(4,2) = 6
- pairs_d1 = n-1 = 3
- degrees: deg(1)=1, deg(2)=2, deg(3)=2, deg(4)=1
- pairs_d2 = C(1,2) + C(2,2) + C(2,2) + C(1,2) = 0 + 1 + 1 + 0 = 2
- New_sum = 2*6 - 3 - 2 = 12 - 5 = 7 ✓

Perfect!

```python
n = int(input())

if n == 2:
    print(1)
else:
    # Build adjacency list
    adj = [[] for _ in range(n + 1)]
    
    for _ in range(n - 1):
        u, v = map(int, input().split())
        adj[u].append(v)
        adj[v].append(u)
    
    # Calculate number of pairs with distance 1 and 2
    pairs_d1 = n - 1  # number of edges
    
    pairs_d2 = 0
    for i in range(1, n + 1):
        deg = len(adj[i])
        pairs_d2 += deg * (deg - 1) // 2
    
    # Total pairs
    total_pairs = n * (n - 1) // 2
    
    # Final answer = 2 * total_pairs - pairs_d1 - pairs_d2
    result = 2 * total_pairs - pairs_d1 - pairs_d2
    print(result)
```
